**Detroit** ([/dɪˈtrɔɪt/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/be/LL-Q1860\_%28eng%29-Flame%2C_not_lame-Detroit.wav/LL-Q1860\_%28eng%29-Flame%2C_not_lame-Detroit.wav.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/b/be/LL-Q1860_%28eng%29-Flame%2C_not_lame-Detroit.wav/LL-Q1860_%28eng%29-Flame%2C_not_lame-Detroit.wav.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup> [*dih-TROYT*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key), locally also [/ˈdiːtrɔɪt/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [*DEE-troyt*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key))[^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9] is the [most populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Michigan) in the U.S. state of [Michigan](18859.md). It is situated on the bank of the [Detroit River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_River) across from the Canadian city of [Windsor, Ontario](33596.md). It is the [26th-most populous city in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population) and the largest U.S. city on the [Canada–United States border](630260.md), with a population of 639,111 at the [2020 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census), and an estimated 2025 population of 649,095, its first population increase since 1950.[^cite_note-QuickFacts-10] The [Metro Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Detroit) area, at over 4.4 million people, is the [14th-largest metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_statistical_area) in the nation and second-largest in the [Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States) (after the [Chicago metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_metropolitan_area)). The [county seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_seat) of [Wayne County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayne_County,_Michigan), Detroit is a significant cultural center known for its contributions to music, art, architecture, and design, in addition to its historical automotive and industrial background.[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12]

In 1701, French explorers [Antoine de la Mothe Cadillac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_de_la_Mothe_Cadillac) and [Alphonse de Tonty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alphonse_de_Tonty) founded [Fort Pontchartrain du Détroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Pontchartrain_du_D%C3%A9troit). During the late 19th and early 20th centuries, it became an important industrial hub at the center of the [Great Lakes region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes_region). The city's population rose to be the fourth-largest in the nation by 1920, with the expansion of the [automotive industry](163778.md) in the early 20th century.[^cite_note-13] One of its main features, the [Detroit River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_River), became the busiest commercial hub in the world. In the mid-20th century, Detroit entered a state of [urban decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_decay) that has continued to the present, as a result of industrial restructuring, the loss of jobs in the auto industry, and rapid suburbanization. Since reaching a peak of 1.85 million at the [1950 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_United_States_census), Detroit's population has declined by more than 65 percent.[^cite_note-QuickFacts-10] In 2013, Detroit [became the largest U.S. city to file for bankruptcy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_bankruptcy), but successfully exited in 2014.[^cite_note-14] In 2025, the U.S. Census Bureau reported that Detroit's population grew for a second consecutive year and led population growth in Michigan for the first time since the 1950s.[^cite_note-Afana-15]

Detroit is a port on the Detroit River, one of the four major [straits](58646.md) that connect the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes) system to the [St. Lawrence Seaway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Lawrence_Seaway). The city anchors the third-largest regional economy in the Midwest and the 16th-largest in the United States.[^cite_note-16] It is also best known as the center of the [U.S. automotive industry](23576520.md), and the "[Big Three](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Three_\(automobile_manufacturers\))" auto manufacturers—[General Motors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Motors), [Ford](30433662.md), and [Stellantis](64563392.md) North America ([Chrysler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysler))—are all headquartered in Metro Detroit.[^cite_note-17] Metro Detroit houses the [Detroit Metropolitan Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Metropolitan_Airport), one of the most important [hub airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airline_hub) in the United States. Detroit and the adjacent Canadian city of [Windsor](33596.md) constitute the [second-busiest international crossing](7598140.md) in North America, after [San Diego–Tijuana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Diego%E2%80%93Tijuana).[^cite_note-18]

[Detroit's culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Detroit) is marked with diversity, having both local and international influences. Detroit gave rise to the [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Detroit) genres of [Motown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motown_\(music_style\)) and [techno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Techno), and also played an important role in the development of [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_jazz), [hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Detroit#Hip-hop), [rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Detroit#Rock), and [punk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Detroit#Hardcore_punk). As a result of the city's rapid growth in its boom years, Detroit has many globally unique [architectural monuments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_metropolitan_Detroit) and [historic places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places_listings_in_Detroit). Since the 2000s, [conservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation-restoration_of_cultural_heritage) efforts have managed to save many architectural pieces and achieve several large-scale [revitalizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planning_and_development_in_Detroit), including the restoration of several historic [theaters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Performing_arts_in_Detroit) and entertainment venues, [high-rise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Detroit) renovations, new sports stadiums, and a riverfront revitalization project. Detroit is an [increasingly popular tourist destination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_metropolitan_Detroit) which caters to about 16 million visitors per year.[^cite_note-19] In 2015, Detroit was designated a "[City of Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Design_Cities_\(UNESCO\))" by [UNESCO](21786641.md), the first and only U.S. city to receive this designation.[^cite_note-20]

## Etymology and nicknames

See also: [Nicknames of Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicknames_of_Detroit)

Detroit is named after the [Detroit River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_River), connecting [Lake St. Clair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_St._Clair) with [Lake Erie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Erie). The name comes from the French language word *détroit* meaning 'strait' as the city was situated on a narrow north–south passage of water linking the two lakes. The river was known as *le détroit du Lac Érié* in the French language, which means 'the strait of Lake Erie'.[^cite_note-21][^cite_note-22] In historical contexts, the strait included Lake St. Clair and the Detroit River.[^cite_note-RivièreduDétroit-23][^cite_note-24]

Throughout its history, Detroit has been known by several nicknames. Its founder, [Antoine de la Mothe Cadillac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_de_la_Mothe_Cadillac), originally envisioned the settlement as a major city within the [Pays d'en Haut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pays_d'en_Haut), calling it the *Paris de la Nouvelle-France*, (*[Paris](22989.md) of [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France)*). As the city evolved into the world's automotive center, "Detroit" became a [metonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metonymy) for the industry itself.[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-Britannica-26] Its status as the heart of the automotive world is reflected in the nickname *Motor City*, while its deep musical legacy is famously celebrated through the moniker *Motown*.[^cite_note-27]

Other nicknames emerged during the 20th century to reflect different aspects of the city's identity. It became known as the *City of Champions* in the 1930s following a series of sports successes, while the [Detroit Red Wings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Red_Wings) later trademarked the name *[Hockeytown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hockeytown)*.[^cite_note-City_of_Champions-28] In more recent years, it has been called the *Renaissance City*, a nod to its urban revitalization efforts and the iconic [Renaissance Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_Center) complex. The city is also frequently referred to as *The D*, *The 313* (its telephone area code),[^cite_note-29] or *Rock City*, inspired by the [Kiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiss_\(band\)) song "[Detroit Rock City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Rock_City)."

## History

Main article: [History of Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Detroit)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Detroit).

### Indigenous history

[Paleo-Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Indians) inhabited areas near Detroit as early as 11,000 years ago including the culture referred to as the [Mound Builders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mound_Builders).[^cite_note-lemke-30] By the 17th century, the region was inhabited by [Huron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huron_people), [Odawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odawa), [Potawatomi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potawatomi), and [Iroquois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iroquois) peoples.[^cite_note-teasdale-31] The area is known by the [Anishinaabe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anishinaabe) people as *Waawiiyaataanong*, translating to 'where the water curves around'.[^cite_note-32]

The first Europeans did not penetrate into the region and reach the straits of Detroit until French [missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionary) and traders worked their way around the Iroquois League, with whom they were at war in the 1630s.[^cite_note-AmHeritageBk-33] The Huron and [Neutral people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_Confederacy) held the north side of Lake Erie until the 1650s, when the Iroquois pushed them and the [Erie people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erie_people) away from the lake and its [beaver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaver)-rich feeder streams in the [Beaver Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaver_Wars) of 1649–1655.[^cite_note-AmHeritageBk-33] By the 1670s, the war-weakened Iroquois laid claim to as far south as the [Ohio River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River) valley in northern [Kentucky](16846.md) as hunting grounds,[^cite_note-AmHeritageBk-33] and had absorbed many other Iroquoian peoples after defeating them in war.[^cite_note-AmHeritageBk-33] For the next hundred years, virtually no British or French action was contemplated without consultation with the Iroquois or consideration of their likely response.[^cite_note-AmHeritageBk-33]

### French era (1701–1760)

Main article: [Fort Pontchartrain du Détroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Pontchartrain_du_D%C3%A9troit)

On July 24, 1701, the French explorer [Antoine de la Mothe Cadillac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_de_la_Mothe_Cadillac) (1658–1730), with his lieutenant [Alphonse de Tonty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alphonse_de_Tonty) (1659–1727), and more than a hundred other [Royal French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France) settlers traveling south and west from [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France) (modern [Province of Quebec](7954867.md)), along the [St. Lawrence River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Lawrence_River) valley to the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes) region, began constructing a small fort on the north bank of the Detroit River. Cadillac named the settlement [Fort Pontchartrain du Détroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Pontchartrain_du_D%C3%A9troit),[^cite_note-34] after [Louis Phélypeaux, comte de Pontchartrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Ph%C3%A9lypeaux,_Marquis_of_Ph%C3%A9lypeaux) (1643–1727), the [Secretary of State of the Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_the_Navy_\(France\)) under King [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV) (1638–1715).[^cite_note-Riley-35] [Sainte-Anne de Détroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_Sainte_Anne_de_D%C3%A9troit) was founded on July 26 and is the second-oldest continuously operating [Roman Catholic](606848.md) parish in the United States.[^cite_note-36] France offered free land to colonists to attract families further west into the Great Lakes region interior of the North American continent to Detroit; when it eventually reached a population of about 800 by 1765, after the colonial conflict of the [French and Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_War) (1753–1763), ([Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War) in Europe), it became the largest European settlement between the important towns of [Montreal](7954681.md) and [New Orleans](53842.md), both also French settlements, in the former colonies of New France and [La Louisiane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\)) (further south on the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River), on the coast of the [Gulf of Mexico](21076367.md)), respectively.[^cite_note-37] The region's then colonial economy was based on the lucrative [fur trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_fur_trade), in which numerous Native American peoples had important roles as trappers and traders.

### British era (1760–1796)

Further information: [Fort Lernoult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Lernoult)



A topographical plan of the town of Detroit prior to the [Great Fire of 1805](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Fire_of_1805), depicting the British [bastion fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bastion_fort) of [Lernoult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Lernoult) (above) and the French era buildings of [Fort Pontchartrain du Détroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Pontchartrain_du_D%C3%A9troit) (below)

During the [French and Indian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_War) (1753–63)—the North American front of the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War) in Europe between the [Kingdom of Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain) and the [Kingdom of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France)—British troops gained control of the settlement a few years into the conflict in 1760 and shortened its name to Detroit. Several regional Native American tribes, such as the [Potowatomi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potawatomi), [Ojibwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ojibwe) and Huron, launched [Pontiac's War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontiac's_War) (1763–1766), and [laid siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Fort_Detroit) in 1763 to [Fort Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Detroit) along the Detroit River in the Great Lakes but failed to capture it. In defeat, France ceded its territory in North America of New France and south of the lakes east of the Mississippi to the Appalachian Mountains to Britain following the war.[^cite_note-38]

When Great Britain evicted France from its colonial possessions in [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France) ([Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_\(New_France\))) in the peace terms of the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1763\)) of 1763, it also removed one barrier to American colonists migrating west across the mountains.[^cite_note-39] British negotiations with the Iroquois would both prove critical and lead to the [Royal Proclamation of 1763](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Proclamation_of_1763), which limited settlements South of and below the Great Lakes and west of the [Alleghenies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alleghenies) / [Appalachians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachians). Many colonists and pioneers in the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies) along the [East Coast](89126.md), resented and then simply defied this restraint, later becoming supporters of the rebellious [American Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution). By 1773, after the addition of increasing numbers of the [Anglo-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Americans) settlers, the population of Detroit and [Fort Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Detroit), was edging up to 1,400 (doubled in the previous decade). During the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War) (1775–1783), the indigenous and loyalist raids of 1778 and the resultant 1779 decisive [Sullivan Expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sullivan_Expedition) reopened the [Ohio Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_Country) (north of the [Ohio River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_River) and west of the mountains) to even more westward emigration, which began almost immediately to get away from the eastern warfare. By 1778, its population had doubled again, reaching 2,144 and it was the third-largest town in what was known then as the [Province of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_\(1763%E2%80%9391\)) since the British takeover of former French colonial possessions in North America in 1763.[^cite_note-40]

After the [American Revolutionary War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War) (1775–1783) and the establishment and recognition of the United States as an independent country, Great Britain ceded Detroit and other territories in the interior region of the continent, south of the Great Lakes and west of the [Appalachian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Mountains) to the [Mississippi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River) under the peace of the terms of the [1783 Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1783\)). The new [Northwest Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Territory) established the southern border with Great Britain's remaining colonial provinces in [British North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_North_America) and became provinces of [Upper Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Canada) and [Lower Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Canada). However, the disputed border area remained under British control with several military forts and trading posts for another decade, and its forces did not fully withdraw until 1796, following the negotiations and ratification of the subsequent [Jay Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Treaty) of 1794 between the British and Americans.[^cite_note-41] By the turn of the 19th century, white American settlers began pouring westwards across the Appalachians and through the Great Lakes.[^cite_note-42]

Today the municipal [flag of Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Detroit) reflects both its French and English colonial heritage. Descendants of the earliest French and [French-Canadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Canadian) settlers formed a cohesive community, who gradually were superseded as the dominant population after more Anglo-American settlers arrived in the early 19th century with American westward migration. Living along the shores of Lake St. Clair and south to [Monroe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe,_Michigan) and downriver suburbs, the ethnic French Canadians of Detroit remain a subculture in the region up into the 21st century.[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44]

### Early American era (1796–1900)







From top: [Woodward Avenue shopping district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Woodward_Avenue_Historic_District) in 1865; *The City of Detroit (from Canada Shore)*, 1872, by A. C. Warren; the [Belle Isle Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belle_Isle_Park) in 1891

After the [Great Fire of 1805](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Fire_of_1805) destroyed nearly all of Detroit's colonial buildings, the town was quickly rebuilt with aid from both local residents and those from [Quebec](7954867.md).[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47] The town of Detroit was first incorporated as a city in 1806, but the incorporation was repealed just three years later. It was reincorporated in 1815 following the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812).[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-49][^cite_note-50] In 1824, the city held its first mayoral election, with [John R. Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_R._Williams) becoming the first elected mayor.[^cite_note-Farmer-51] Following Michigan's admission to the Union in 1837, Detroit served as the state's first capital and hosted the inaugural [state elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Michigan) that same year.[^cite_note-52]

Between 1815 and 1900, the city borders expanded significantly through the annexation of land from surrounding townships, such as [Hamtramck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamtramck_Township,_Michigan), [Springwells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springwells_Township,_Michigan), [Greenfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenfield_Township,_Michigan), and [Grosse Pointe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grosse_Pointe_Township,_Michigan), before many of those townships incorporated as independent cities in the 20th century.[^cite_note-Farmer-51] [Public land sales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privatization_of_public_land_\(United_States\)) began in 1818, and by 1836, the city formalized its public water supply by establishing a [municipal water works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Water_and_Sewerage_Department).[^cite_note-53] It followed the end of the [second cholera pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1826%E2%80%931837_cholera_pandemic) that had devastated Detroit and many other North American cities.[^cite_note-54]

The city's population grew steadily throughout the 19th century, fueled by immigrants from [Germany](11867.md), [Poland](22936.md), [Italy](14532.md), [Greece](12108.md), and [Belgium](3343.md).[^cite_note-55] By the 1830s, [Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Americans) had become the city's predominant ethnic group. During the [Irish Great Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Famine_\(Ireland\)) of the 1840s, a wave of [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Americans) immigrants arrived, settling primarily in the [Corktown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corktown,_Detroit) neighborhood.[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57] Detroit's Black population remained small during the 19th century; it constituted less than four percent of the city's population by the time of the [1863 race riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_race_riot_of_1863) during the [Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War), numbering roughly 1,500 to 1,600 residents.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59][^cite_note-Chadwick-60][^cite_note-61]

Detroit's unique geography provided easy access to raw materials like [iron ore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_mining_in_the_United_States), [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper_mining_in_Michigan), [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_mining_in_the_United_States), and [timber](38922691.md).[^cite_note-62] Coupled with the opening of the [Erie Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erie_Canal) in 1825, this proximity shifted the city's local economy toward heavy industries such as [machinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machinery), [metalworking](266443.md), and [engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engine) production in the mid-19th century.[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64] Well before the automotive era, the city led the nation in the construction of [railroad cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railroad_cars) and [freighters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_freighter); the [Michigan-Peninsular Car Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan-Peninsular_Car_Company) was the city's largest manufacturer during that period, while the [Detroit Dry Dock Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dry_Dock_Complex_\(Detroit\)) built hulls for the largest ships on the Great Lakes.[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66] By the 1870s, [cast-iron stoves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood-burning_stove) manufacturing had become Detroit's largest single industry.[^cite_note-67] The city also became a major producer of [pharmaceuticals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceuticals), [seeds](242778.md), [tobacco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tobacco_in_the_United_States), and [beer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_in_the_United_States), with companies like the [Ferry-Morse Seed Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferry-Morse_Seed_Company) and pharmaceutical companies [Parke-Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parke-Davis) and [Frederick Stearns & Co.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Stearns_Building) emerging as national industry leaders.[^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69] The [Detroit International Exposition and Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_International_Exposition_and_Fair_of_1889) (1889–1892) showcased the city's expanding economy in the late 19th century.[^cite_note-70]

### Early 20th century







From top: [Cadillac Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadillac_Square) and [Wayne County Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayne_County_Building) (1902); [Cadillac Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campus_Martius_Park) (1910s); corner of [Michigan Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Route_12_in_Michigan) and [Griswold Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Griswold_Street) (circa 1920)

Detroit saw explosive growth in the [auto industry](163778.md) in the early 20th century. In 1913, [Henry Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Ford)'s [moving assembly line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_line) revolutionized automotive manufacturing, reducing [chassis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chassis) assembly time by nearly 90%.[^cite_note-Wilson2014-71] This efficiency enabled low-cost [mass production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_production), with a new [Model T](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Model_T) rolling off the line every 24 seconds at peak capacity.[^cite_note-Wilson2014-71] By 1920, Detroit's automakers, including [Ford](30433662.md), [Buick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buick) (GM), [Chevrolet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevrolet) (GM), [Dodge Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodge_Brothers) (Chrysler), and [Maxwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxwell_Motor_Company) (Chrysler), were producing one million cars annually, accounting for roughly half of the world's total production.[^cite_note-Sugrue-72] The city was undoubtedly the automotive capital of the world.[^cite_note-Sugrue-72]

Even before the rise of the auto industry, Detroit was already a primary gateway for [iron ore](145352.md), [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal), and [grain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grain) moving through the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes).[^cite_note-73] Its [shipping tonnage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipping_ton) frequently exceeded that of the world's major [saltwater ports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port); in 1907 alone, the [Detroit River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_River) carried over 67 million tons of cargo, surpassing that of the ports of [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_London) (18.7 million) and [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_New_York_and_New_Jersey) (20.4 million) combined.[^cite_note-74] The subsequent surge in auto production after 1914 fueled a parallel expansion in the city's commerce. However, the increasing traffic at the [Detroit-Windsor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit-Windsor) crossing frequently caused bottlenecks, as railroads and automobiles still relied on slow [ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferries_in_Michigan).[^cite_note-75] The opening of the [Michigan Central Railway Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_Central_Railway_Tunnel), the [Ambassador Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambassador_Bridge), and the [Detroit–Windsor Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit%E2%80%93Windsor_Tunnel) largely replaced these ferries, significantly reducing the shipment and storage costs of essential commodities.[^cite_note-76]

Between 1900 and 1930, the city's population more than quintupled, soaring from roughly 285,000 to 1.6 million. By 1950, Detroit's population peaked at 1.85 million, making it the fifth-largest city in the U.S.[^cite_note-77] At the time, residents enjoyed the highest [per capita income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita_income) in the country, driven by high-paying manufacturing jobs in the booming automotive industry.[^cite_note-78] In fact, a typical Ford worker earned an annual wage more than twice the national median.[^cite_note-79] During this era, Detroit also maintained a lower poverty rate than its suburbs. With a median household income approximately 20% above the national number and one of the highest [homeownership rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeownership_in_the_United_States) in the country, Detroit was the wealthiest city in the U.S. and, by some accounts, had the highest [standard of living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_of_living) in the world.[^cite_note-80]

The [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) hit Detroit hard as car sales plummeted, leading to mass unemployment. The city saw the formation of several labor unions, notably the [United Auto Workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Auto_Workers), founded in Detroit in 1935.[^cite_note-81] Around this same time, the [Teamsters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teamsters) strengthened their Detroit operations in the early 1930s, largely through [James Hoffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Hoffa)'s organizing of warehouse and freight drivers.[^cite_note-82] As the city emerged from the Depression, racial tensions escalated between white residents and Black Southerners who had arrived during the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_\(African_American\)).[^cite_note-DetroitDivided-83][^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85] This friction, fueled by intense competition for jobs and housing,[^cite_note-86] eventually culminated in the [1943 race riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1943_Detroit_race_riot).[^cite_note-87]

During [World War II](32927.md), the U.S. government encouraged the retooling of the auto industry to support the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II).[^cite_note-Baime14-88] Civilian car production ceased from 1942 to 1945 as factories shifted to manufacturing military equipment.[^cite_note-Baime14-88] Despite having only 2% of the U.S. population, the city produced over 10% of all American war materiel, while the broader Detroit metro region accounted for nearly 30% of the nation's total output.[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90] This staggering output was led by the [Detroit Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Assembly) and the [Detroit Tank Arsenal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Tank_Arsenal), which together produced nearly as many tanks as all of [Nazi Germany](21212.md).[^cite_note-91]



The skyline of [Downtown Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Detroit) in 1929

### Late 20th century

#### Decline of the "Model City"

Main article: [Decline of Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_Detroit)





The [Hudson Motor Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Motor_Car_Company) (above) and the [Packard Automotive Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Packard_Automotive_Plant) (below) were among many Detroit auto factories that closed during the industrial deconcentration in the 1950s.

The first signs of Detroit's decline appeared in the 1950s with the onset of [industrial deconcentration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_deconcentration) and [suburbanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburbanization).[^cite_note-92] The [Packard Automotive Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Packard_Automotive_Plant) closed during that decade, and the massive [Hudson Motor Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Motor_Car_Company) at East Jefferson and Conner was largely demolished by 1961. [Studebaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Studebaker) ended its manufacturing presence in Detroit in 1956, and [Kaiser-Frazer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaiser-Frazer) ceased local passenger car production in the mid-1950s.[^cite_note-93]

In 1961, under the [Louis Miriani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Miriani) administration, the city recorded its first post-war budget deficit of \$16 million.[^cite_note-94] This shortfall resulted from a sharp decline in tax revenue as major industries and residents moved to the suburbs. Since its 1950 peak of 1.85 million, Detroit has lost 65% of its population.[^cite_note-95]

As the city's overall population declined, its Black population nearly doubled between 1950 and 1970, and the city officially became [majority-Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority_minority_in_the_United_States) in the 1970s. Most migrated from the [Deep South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_South), drawn by auto industry jobs. However, these newcomers were often relegated to lower-paying roles, while higher-paying positions remained dominated by longtime white residents. Furthermore, discriminatory [redlining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redlining) policies frequently denied them access to essential financial services.[^cite_note-tjs1-96][^cite_note-Meinke2011-97][^cite_note-Sedler87-98][^cite_note-99] This era was defined by the [civil rights movement](49001.md); notably, in June 1963, Dr. [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md) delivered a major speech during the [Detroit Walk to Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Walk_to_Freedom) that foreshadowed his "[I Have a Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Have_a_Dream)" speech in [Washington, D.C.](108956.md), just two months later.[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-Detroit_Speech-101] Despite the passage of [federal civil rights legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964), deep-seated racial tensions ignited the [12th Street Riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Detroit_riot) in 1967, which remains one of the deadliest and costliest civil disturbances in U.S. history.[^cite_note-102]





A photo comparison spanning 74 years, showing the decline of [Downtown Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Detroit): the [First Williams Block](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Avenue_Commercial_Buildings) on Monroe Street in 1915 (left) vs. 1989 (right)

In 1973, [Coleman Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coleman_Young) was elected as Detroit's first Black mayor. Throughout his 20-year tenure, the city's economy continued to deteriorate as businesses and residents fled.[^cite_note-Bailey-103][^cite_note-104] Early in his first term, Young faced a looming "near-bankruptcy" situation.[^cite_note-Peterson1981-105] To maintain a balanced budget, he implemented aggressive tax increases and spending cuts.[^cite_note-Peterson1981-105] While these policies were intended to ensure the city's solvency, critics often argue they actually accelerated the exodus of businesses and residents, creating a "financial death spiral." As income tax rates rose to cover budget gaps, the resulting departure of taxpayers inadvertently led to further deficits.[^cite_note-106]

During the [recession of the early 1980s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_1980s_recession), triggered by the [1979 oil crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_oil_crisis), the automotive industry suffered massive layoffs, leaving Detroit with a \$119 million deficit by 1981.[^cite_note-Peterson1981-105] To avoid bankruptcy, Young successfully campaigned for an [income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax_in_the_United_States) hike and sold up to \$125 million in emergency bonds. By his final year in office in 1993–94, the city faced a \$271 million deficit. To address this, Young mandated a 10% salary cut for city employees before leaving office.[^cite_note-107]

Detroit has lacked high-capacity [mass transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_transit) since 1956, when its primary streetcar system was dismantled.[^cite_note-Peter_Gavrilovich_2000_p.232-108][^cite_note-109] Following mounting pressure for rapid transit, a 1972 study recommended a system connecting [Downtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Detroit), [Midtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Detroit), and suburbs like [Dearborn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dearborn,_Michigan), [Southfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southfield,_Michigan), and the [Detroit Metro Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Metro_Airport).[^cite_note-110] In 1976, U.S. President [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Ford) offered the Detroit region \$600 million in federal funding to build a robust mass transit system that included a [subway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) and suburban [commuter rail](6286.md).[^cite_note-Felton2014-111] However, local disagreements over project details and regional control delayed implementation for years, leading the [Reagan administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_administration) to withdraw the funding in the early 1980s.[^cite_note-Felton2014-111] By 1987, only the 2.9-mile [People Mover loop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_People_Mover) was completed, serving as a fragment of the originally envisioned broader network.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113]

[Dennis Archer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Archer) became Detroit's 67th mayor in January 1994, succeeding Coleman Young.[^cite_note-Kleiman1995-114] While the city tax base continued to shrink with a declining population, the Archer administration was able to revitalize its finances.[^cite_note-Kleiman1995-114] Inheriting an \$88 million deficit, Archer balanced the budget within his first year through rigorous fiscal reforms. He streamlined the payroll by eliminating hundreds of municipal positions through attrition and deployed "turnaround teams" of outside business experts to improve city services.[^cite_note-Kleiman1995-114] Under the Archer administration, Detroit stabilized its credit rating and achieved a streak of consistent budget surpluses.[^cite_note-Kleiman1995-114]

To diversify Detroit's tax base and retain revenue that was otherwise flowing to the nearby city of [Windsor](33596.md), a [statewide initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_Gaming_Control_and_Revenue_Act) was proposed and passed in 1996 to allow [casino gambling in Detroit](3596847.md), leading to the opening of three privately owned casinos in the downtown area.[^cite_note-115]

### 21st century

#### Bankruptcy

Main article: [Detroit bankruptcy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_bankruptcy)



In 2012, [Belle Isle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belle_Isle_Park) suffered from an estimated \$300 million in deferred maintenance due to the city's budget crisis. The park was leased to the state following the 2013 bankruptcy filing.

Starting in the mid-2000s, the city relied heavily on borrowing to address persistent budget gaps and pension liabilities, including \$1.4 billion in 2005 alone.[^cite_note-\:demos-116] This initiated a seven-year streak of annual [deficits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deficit_spending), each exceeding \$150 million.[^cite_note-117] The then-Mayor, [Kwame Kilpatrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwame_Kilpatrick), was forced to resign in 2008 after felony convictions, and was later sentenced to 28 years in federal prison.[^cite_note-npr.org-118][^cite_note-fed_sentence-119] His actions were estimated to have cost the city approximately \$20 million.[^cite_note-120]

Between 2000 and 2010, the [U.S. Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Census) recorded Detroit's largest single-decade population drop in its history, at approximately 25%. This severe population decline resulted in a massive reduction in state [revenue sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenue_sharing), as constitutional revenue sharing is distributed on a [per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita) basis. Furthermore, foreclosures caused by unemployment reduced property values across the city following the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession), further reducing the tax base.[^cite_note-121] In 2011, the city government recorded that roughly half of its 305,000 property owners failed to pay their taxes, leaving over \$246 million uncollected.[^cite_note-122] By 2013, Detroit faced a \$327 million budget deficit and more than \$18 billion in total long-term debt and liabilities.[^cite_note-123]

Facing a financial crisis, then-Mayor [Dave Bing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Bing) and the City Council accepted state oversight in exchange for financial aid from the state.[^cite_note-124] In March 2013, Michigan Governor [Rick Snyder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Snyder) declared a [financial emergency in Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_emergency_in_Michigan), leading the Local Emergency Financial Assistance Loan Board to appoint [Kevyn Orr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevyn_Orr) as the city’s emergency manager.[^cite_note-125] After negotiations for cuts failed, Orr announced on June 14 that Detroit would default on approximately \$2.5 billion in unsecured debt and skipped a \$39.7 million interest payment.[^cite_note-Detroit_DebtDefault-126] On July 18, 2013, Detroit became the largest U.S. city by population to file for [Chapter 9 bankruptcy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapter_9_bankruptcy).[^cite_note-detroitnews1-127][^cite_note-bankrupt_city-128]

#### Post-bankruptcy (2014-present)

In 2014, [Mike Duggan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Duggan), the former CEO of the [Detroit Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Medical_Center), was elected mayor as the city exited bankruptcy in December, having cut \$7 billion in debt and invested \$1.7 billion in services.[^cite_note-NYTDet-129] To reduce operating costs between 2012 and 2015, Detroit cut its full-time workforce by nearly 40%.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-crcmich-2-131] It also eliminated annual maintenance and operation expenses for [Belle Isle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belle_Isle_Park) by leasing the park to the state.[^cite_note-freep-2013-132] The lease is set for 30 years, with the option for two 15-year renewals.[^cite_note-freep-2013-132] Additionally, the [Detroit Institute of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Institute_of_Arts) became a private organization to help fund the city's recovery following legal battles.[^cite_note-133]

Following its bankruptcy exit, Detroit has stabilized its finances with a string of [budget surpluses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balanced_budget) under the Duggan administration.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-City_of_Detroit-135] The city now maintains roughly \$550 million in reserves.[^cite_note-City_of_Detroit-135] These funds are earmarked for the city’s [rainy day fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainy_day_fund), retiree protection, and various liability funds to mitigate potential [economic slumps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_collapse).[^cite_note-City_of_Detroit-135] The city resumed payments to its two [pension funds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pension_funds) in 2024, ending a nine-year period during which the state of Michigan and private organizations handled those contributions.[^cite_note-crcmich-2-131]

Since 2014, the city's neighborhoods have witnessed the demolition of over 27,000 dangerous, long-abandoned residential structures that had become hotbeds for criminal activity.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] Initiatives such as "ShotStoppers," which mobilizes local community groups to reduce violence in their neighborhoods,[^cite_note-138] along with other violence intervention programs and youth homelessness initiatives, have provided [mental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_health) support, [job placement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Job_placement), and [housing assistance](177912.md) to at-risk youth, contributing to a decline in the city's [crime rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_rate).[^cite_note-139] Additionally, efforts have been made to enhance city services, including improvements to [emergency response times](146330.md),[^cite_note-Ferretti2018-140] [bus services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport_bus_service),[^cite_note-Ferretti2018-140] and the replacement of tens of thousands of non-functional streetlights with [LED light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LED_street_light).[^cite_note-141]





[Michigan Central Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_Central_Station) in 2010 (left) before its renovation, and in 2025 (right) after [Ford Motor Company](30433662.md) undertook extensive renovations to transform the historic site into the company's [Corktown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corktown,_Detroit) campus.

[^cite_note-142]

[Downtown Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Detroit), which had long been neglected, has seen numerous new developments through [public-private partnerships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public-private_partnerships). Since 2014, many abandoned or underutilized landmark buildings in the downtown area have been revitalized through the city's subsidized programs and private investments.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144] The city's [streetcar system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streetcars_in_North_America), [QLine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QLine), opened for public use in May 2017.[^cite_note-145] The construction of [Little Caesars Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Caesars_Arena) and [Hudson's Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson's_Detroit) has attracted many [small businesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_and_medium_enterprises), including shops and restaurants along [Woodward Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodward_Avenue).[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] The long-vacant [Michigan Central Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_Central_Station) was purchased by the [Ford Motor Company](30433662.md) in 2018 and is being redeveloped into the company's new campus, driving the revitalization of the surrounding [Corktown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corktown,_Detroit) area.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152]

After more than half a century of population decline, the city experienced its first population growth since the late 1950s in 2024, recording a higher growth rate than any other city in the state.[^cite_note-153] In 2025, the [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) reported that Detroit's population had increased for the second consecutive year.[^cite_note-Afana-15][^cite_note-154]

In January 2026, [Mary Sheffield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Sheffield), the former president of the [Detroit City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_City_Council), was sworn in as the [city's 76th mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mayors_of_Detroit).[^cite_note-Altavena-155] She is the first woman to be elected to the office.[^cite_note-Altavena-155]

While Detroit has seen gains in city finances and [investor confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_value#Economic_value_and_investor_confidence), it still lags significantly behind national standards for income and education. It remains the poorest [large city in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population), with half of its children living in poverty.[^cite_note-156] In 2024, the city's median household income was less than half the national number.[^cite_note-157] Education remains a critical challenge; despite having high [per-pupil funding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_school_funding_in_the_United_States),[^cite_note-158] the [Detroit Public Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Public_Schools_Community_District) ranked last among 26 large urban districts.[^cite_note-159] Proficiency in reading and math stands at less than 10% for both fourth and eighth graders,[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162] and 61% of students were [chronically absent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absenteeism) during the 2024–25 school year.[^cite_note-163]

## Geography

### Topography



A satellite image from Sentinel-2 of Detroit and its surrounding metropolitan area with Windsor, Ontario across the river, September 2021

According to the [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau), the city has a total area of 142.87 square miles (370.03 km<sup>2</sup>), of which 138.75 square miles (359.36 km<sup>2</sup>) is land and 4.12 square miles (10.67 km<sup>2</sup>) is water.[^cite_note-Gazetteer_files-164] Detroit is the principal city in [Metro Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Detroit) and [Southeast Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Michigan). It is situated in the [Midwestern United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States) and the [Great Lakes region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes_region).[^cite_note-165]

The [Detroit River International Wildlife Refuge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_River_International_Wildlife_Refuge) is the only international [wildlife preserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_preserve) in North America and is uniquely located in the heart of a major metropolitan area. The refuge includes islands, coastal wetlands, marshes, shoals, and waterfront lands along 48 miles (77 km) of the Detroit River and western Lake Erie shoreline.[^cite_note-166]

The city slopes gently from the northwest to southeast on a [till plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Till_plain) composed largely of glacial and lake clay. The most notable topographical feature in the city is the Detroit Moraine, a broad clay ridge on which the older portions of Detroit and Windsor are located, rising approximately 62 feet (19 m) above the river at its highest point.[^cite_note-167] The highest elevation in the city is directly north of Gorham Playground on the northwest side approximately three blocks south of [8 Mile Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M-102_\(Michigan_highway\)), at a height of 675 to 680 feet (206 to 207 m).[^cite_note-168] Detroit's lowest elevation is along the Detroit River, at a surface height of 572 feet (174 m).[^cite_note-169]

### Cityscape

#### Architecture

See also: [List of tallest buildings in Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Detroit)



The [Guardian Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardian_Building) was designated a [National Historic Landmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Historic_Landmark) in 1989.

Detroit's waterfront showcases a variety of architectural styles, with the [postmodern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodern_architecture) [Neo-Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_Revival_architecture) spires of [Ally Detroit Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ally_Detroit_Center) paying homage to the city's [Art Deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Deco) skyscrapers. Together with the [Renaissance Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_Center), these buildings form a distinctive and recognizable skyline. Examples of the Art Deco style include the [Guardian Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guardian_Building) and [Penobscot Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penobscot_Building) downtown, as well as the [Fisher Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fisher_Building) and [Cadillac Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadillac_Place) in [New Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Center,_Detroit). Prominent cultural landmarks from the early 20th century include the [Fox Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Theatre_\(Detroit\)), [Detroit Opera House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Opera_House), and [Detroit Institute of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Institute_of_Arts).[^cite_note-AIADetroit-170][^cite_note-DetArch-171]

While [Downtown Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Detroit) and New Center feature high-rise buildings, much of Detroit consists of low-rise structures and single-family homes. Residential high-rises are concentrated in upscale neighborhoods such as the East Riverfront, extending toward [Grosse Pointe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grosse_Pointe), and [Palmer Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmer_Park_Apartment_Building_Historic_District). The University Commons-Palmer Park district anchors historic areas including [Palmer Woods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmer_Woods), [Sherwood Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherwood_Forest,_Detroit), and the [University District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_District,_Detroit) near the [University of Detroit Mercy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Detroit_Mercy).[^cite_note-172]

42 significant structures in the city are [listed on the National Register of Historic Places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Registered_Historic_Places_in_Detroit,_Michigan). Pre-World War II neighborhoods exhibit architectural styles of the era, with working-class areas featuring wood-frame and brick houses, while middle- and upper-class neighborhoods such as [Brush Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brush_Park), [Woodbridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodbridge,_Detroit), [Indian Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Village,_Detroit), Palmer Woods, and [Boston-Edison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston-Edison_Historic_District) contain larger, more ornate homes and mansions.[^cite_note-173] Multi-million dollar restorations and new developments have revitalized neighborhoods such as [West Canfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Canfield_Historic_District) and Brush Park.[^cite_note-partnership-174][^cite_note-175]

The city has one of the United States' largest surviving collections of late 19th- and early 20th-century buildings.[^cite_note-DetArch-171] Architecturally significant churches and cathedrals in the city include [St. Joseph's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Joseph_Roman_Catholic_Church,_Detroit), Old [St. Mary's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Mary_Roman_Catholic_Church_\(Detroit\)), the [Sweetest Heart of Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweetest_Heart_of_Mary_Roman_Catholic_Church), and the [Cathedral of the Most Blessed Sacrament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_the_Most_Blessed_Sacrament).[^cite_note-AIADetroit-170] Historic preservation efforts continue to thrive, with downtown redevelopment projects revitalizing parts of the city, among them [Campus Martius Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campus_Martius_Park), [Grand Circus Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Circus_Park_Historic_District) near the [city's theater district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Theatre_District), [Ford Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ford_Field), [Comerica Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comerica_Park), and [Little Caesars Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Caesars_Arena).[^cite_note-AIADetroit-170][^cite_note-Gallagher-176][^cite_note-177]

#### Neighborhoods

Further information: [List of neighborhoods in Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_neighborhoods_in_Detroit)



Typical Detroit [Arts and Crafts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arts_and_Crafts)-style brick houses in the [Atkinson Avenue Historic District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atkinson_Avenue_Historic_District)



[Single-family homes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-family_homes) in the [Rosedale Park Historic District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosedale_Park,_Detroit)

Detroit has a variety of neighborhood types. The revitalized Downtown, [Midtown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midtown_Detroit), Corktown, [New Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Center,_Detroit) areas feature many historic buildings and are high density, while further out, particularly in the northeast and on the fringes,[^cite_note-parcel-178] high vacancy levels are problematic, for which a number of solutions have been proposed. In 2007, [Downtown Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Detroit) was recognized as the best city neighborhood in which to retire among the United States' largest metro areas by [CNNMoney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNNMoney) editors.[^cite_note-179]

[Lafayette Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lafayette_Park,_Detroit) is a [revitalized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planning_and_development_in_Detroit) neighborhood on the city's [east side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Jefferson_Avenue_Residential_TR), part of the [Ludwig Mies van der Rohe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_Mies_van_der_Rohe) residential district.[^cite_note-WallStreet1-180] The 78-acre (32 ha) development was originally called the Gratiot Park. Planned by [Mies van der Rohe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mies_van_der_Rohe), [Ludwig Hilberseimer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_Hilberseimer) and [Alfred Caldwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Caldwell) it includes a landscaped, 19-acre (7.7 ha) park with no through traffic, in which these and other low-rise apartment buildings are situated.[^cite_note-WallStreet1-180] Immigrants have contributed to the city's neighborhood revitalization, especially in southwest Detroit.[^cite_note-Southwest2-181] Southwest Detroit has experienced a thriving economy in recent years, as evidenced by new housing, increased business openings and the recently opened [Mexicantown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexicantown,_Detroit) International Welcome Center.[^cite_note-Southwest-182]

The city has numerous neighborhoods consisting of vacant properties resulting in low inhabited density in those areas, stretching city services and infrastructure. These neighborhoods are concentrated in the northeast and on the city's fringes.[^cite_note-parcel-178] A 2009 parcel survey found about a quarter of residential lots in the city to be undeveloped or vacant, and about 10% of the city's housing to be unoccupied.[^cite_note-parcel-178][^cite_note-MLIVEAP-183][^cite_note-Gallagher1-184] The survey also reported that most (86%) of the city's homes are in good condition with a minority (9%) in fair condition needing only minor repairs.[^cite_note-MLIVEAP-183][^cite_note-Gallagher1-184][^cite_note-185][^cite_note-parcel2-186]

To deal with vacancy issues, the city has begun demolishing the derelict houses, razing 3,000 of the total 10,000 in 2010,[^cite_note-187] but the resulting low density creates a strain on the city's infrastructure. To remedy this, a number of solutions have been proposed including resident relocation from more sparsely populated neighborhoods and converting unused space to urban agricultural use, including [Hantz Woodlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hantz_Woodlands), though the city expects to be in the planning stages for up to another two years.[^cite_note-NextDetroit-188][^cite_note-189]

Public funding and private investment have been made with promises to rehabilitate neighborhoods. In April 2008, the city announced a \$300 million (\~\$428 million in 2024) stimulus plan to create jobs and revitalize neighborhoods, financed by city bonds and paid for by earmarking about 15% of the wagering tax.[^cite_note-NextDetroit-188] The city's working plans for neighborhood revitalizations include 7-Mile/Livernois, [Brightmoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brightmoor,_Detroit), East English Village, Grand River/Greenfield, [North End](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_End,_Detroit), and [Osborn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osborn,_Detroit).[^cite_note-NextDetroit-188] Private organizations have pledged substantial funding to the efforts.[^cite_note-DEGA2-190][^cite_note-Fund-191] Additionally, the city has cleared a 1,200-acre (490 ha) section of land for large-scale neighborhood construction, which the city is calling the *Far Eastside Plan*.[^cite_note-Rose1-192] In 2011, Mayor [Dave Bing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Bing) announced a plan to categorize neighborhoods by their needs and prioritize the most needed services for those neighborhoods.[^cite_note-193]

### Monuments and parks

See also: [List of public art in Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_public_art_in_Detroit)



The [Horace E. Dodge and Son Memorial Fountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horace_E._Dodge_and_Son_Memorial_Fountain) in [Hart Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hart_Plaza)

Detroit Parks & Recreation maintains 308 public parks, covering 4,950 acres (2,003 ha), or about 5.6% of the city's land area. [Grand Circus Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Circus_Park), established in 1847 as part of the original Woodward plan, was the city's first official municipal park.[^cite_note-194] Other major parks include [Campus Martius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campus_Martius_Park),[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197] [Belle Isle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belle_Isle_Park),[^cite_note-198] [River Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Rouge_Park), [Palmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmer_Park_\(Detroit\)), and [Chene Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chene_Park).[^cite_note-199] The [Detroit International Riverfront](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_International_Riverfront) also serves as a key recreation space, featuring a 3.5-mile promenade from [Hart Plaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hart_Plaza) to Belle Isle, with a second phase planned to extend the promenade to the [Ambassador Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambassador_Bridge).[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201]

In the surrounding Metro Area, the [Huron–Clinton Metroparks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huron%E2%80%93Clinton_Metroparks) system (established in 1940) includes 13 [regional parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_park). These span over 24,000 acres (97 km<sup>2</sup>) along the [Huron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huron_River_\(Michigan\)) and [Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_River) Rivers.&#x20;

Detroit is also renowned for its outdoor [public art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_art). Many of its fountains serve as monumental landmarks, including the [Bagley Memorial Fountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagley_Memorial_Fountain), the [Horace E. Dodge and Son Memorial Fountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horace_E._Dodge_and_Son_Memorial_Fountain) in Hart Plaza, and the [James Scott Memorial Fountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Scott_Memorial_Fountain) on Belle Isle.

An important civic sculpture is *[The Spirit of Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Spirit_of_Detroit)* by [Marshall Fredericks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Fredericks) at the Coleman Young Municipal Center. The image is often used as a symbol of Detroit, and the statue is occasionally dressed in sports jerseys to celebrate when a Detroit team is doing well.[^cite_note-202] A [memorial to Joe Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monument_to_Joe_Louis) is located at the intersection of Jefferson and Woodward Avenues. The sculpture, commissioned by *[Sports Illustrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_Illustrated)* and executed by [Robert Graham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Graham_\(sculptor\)), is a 24-foot (7.3 m) long arm with a fist suspended by a pyramidal framework.

### Climate

| Metric conversion  J F M A M J J A S O N D        57     0 −7       53     2 −6       62     8 −2       83     15 4       94     21 10       83     27 16       89     29 18       83     28 17       82     24 13       64     17 7       65     9 1       57     3 −4   █ Average max. and min. temperatures in °C █ Precipitation totals in mm |

Detroit and the rest of southeastern Michigan have a hot-summer [humid continental climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): *Dfa*) which is influenced by the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes) like other places in the [state](18859.md);[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205] the city and close-in suburbs are part of USDA [Hardiness zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardiness_zone) 6b, while the more distant northern and western suburbs generally are included in zone 6a.[^cite_note-206] Winters are cold, with moderate snowfall and temperatures not rising above freezing on an average 44 days annually, while dropping to or below 0 °F (−18 °C) on an average 4.4 days a year; summers are warm to hot with temperatures exceeding 90 °F (32 °C) on 12 days.[^cite_note-NOAA-D-207] The warm season runs from May to September. The monthly daily mean temperature ranges from 25.6 °F (−3.6 °C) in January to 73.6 °F (23.1 °C) in July. Official temperature extremes range from 105 °F (41 °C) on July 24, 1934, down to −21 °F (−29 °C) on January 21, 1984; the record low maximum is −4 °F (−20 °C) on [January 19, 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_North_American_cold_wave), while, conversely the record high minimum is 80 °F (27 °C) on August 1, 2006, the most recent of five occurrences.[^cite_note-NOAA-D-207] A decade or two may pass between readings of 100 °F (38 °C) or higher, which last occurred [July 17, 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summer_2012_North_American_heat_wave). The average window for freezing temperatures is October 20 through April 22, allowing a growing season of 180 days.[^cite_note-NOAA-D-207]

Precipitation is moderate and somewhat evenly distributed throughout the year, although the warmer months such as May and June average more, averaging 33.5 inches (850 mm) annually, but historically ranging from 20.49 in (520 mm) in 1963 to 47.70 in (1,212 mm) in 2011.[^cite_note-NOAA-D-207] Snowfall, which typically falls in measurable amounts between November 15 through April 4 (occasionally in October and very rarely in May),[^cite_note-NOAA-D-207] averages 42.5 inches (108 cm) per season, although historically ranging from 11.5 in (29 cm) in 1881–82 to 94.9 in (241 cm) in [2013–14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013%E2%80%9314_North_American_cold_wave).[^cite_note-NOAA-D-207] A thick layer of snow is not often seen, with an average of only 27.5 days with 3 in (7.6 cm) or more of snow cover.[^cite_note-NOAA-D-207] [Thunderstorms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thunderstorms) are frequent in the Detroit area. These usually occur during spring and summer.[^cite_note-208]

Detroit is the center of a three-county urban area (with a population of 3,776,890 within an area of 1,337 square miles (3,460 km<sup>2</sup>) according to the 2020 census), six-county [metropolitan statistical area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_statistical_area) (population of 5,322,219 in an area of 3,913 square miles \[10,130 km<sup>2</sup>] as of the 2010 census), and a nine-county [Combined Statistical Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_Statistical_Area) (population of 5.3 million within 5,814 square miles \[15,060 km<sup>2</sup>] as of 2010).[^cite_note-CensusCSA-217][^cite_note-CensusMSA-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220]

In the [2020 United States census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_census), the city had 639,111 residents, ranking it the [27th-most populous city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_cities_by_population) in the US.[^cite_note-CensusCity-221][^cite_note-NYT_Census-222] Of the large [shrinking cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrinking_cities) in the US, Detroit has had the most dramatic decline in the population of the past 70 years (down 1,210,457) and the second-largest percentage decline (down 65.4%). While the drop in Detroit's population has been ongoing since 1950, the most dramatic period was the significant 25% decline between the [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_census) and 2010 census.[^cite_note-NYT_Census-222]

The population density was 5,144.3 people per square mile (1,986.2 people/km<sup>2</sup>). There were 349,170 housing units at an average density of 2,516.5 units per square mile (971.6 units/km<sup>2</sup>).

### Race, ethnicity, and religion



Ethnic origins in Detroit



Map of racial distribution in Detroit, 2010 U.S. Census. Each dot is 25 people: ⬤ White ⬤ Black ⬤ Asian ⬤ Hispanic ⬤ Other

Beginning with the rise of the automobile industry, Detroit's population increased more than sixfold during the first half of the 20th century as an influx of European ([Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Americans), [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Americans)), Middle Eastern ([Lebanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Americans), [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_Americans)), and Southern migrants brought their families to the city.[^cite_note-223] With this economic boom following World War I, the African American population grew from a mere 6,000 in 1910[^cite_note-224] to more than 120,000 by 1930.[^cite_note-225] Perhaps one of the most overt examples of neighborhood discrimination occurred in 1925 when African American physician [Ossian Sweet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ossian_Sweet) found his home surrounded by an angry mob of his hostile white neighbors violently protesting his new move into a traditionally white neighborhood. Sweet and ten of his family members and friends were put on trial for murder as one of the mob members throwing rocks at the newly purchased house was shot and killed by someone firing out of a second-floor window.[^cite_note-226]

Detroit has a relatively large Mexican-American population. In the early 20th century, thousands of Mexicans came to Detroit to work in agricultural, automotive, and steel jobs. During the [Mexican Repatriation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Repatriation) of the 1930s many Mexicans in Detroit were willingly repatriated or forced to repatriate. By the 1940s much of the Mexican community began to settle what is now [Mexicantown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexicantown,_Detroit).[^cite_note-227] Immigration from [Jalisco](16020434.md) significantly increased the Latino population in the 1990s. By 2010 Detroit had 48,679 Hispanics, including 36,452 Mexicans: a 70% increase from 1990.[^cite_note-Denvir-228] Per the 2023 [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey) five-year estimates, the [Mexican American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_American) population was 35,273 comprising over 75% of the Latino population with [Puerto Ricans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stateside_Puerto_Ricans) as the next largest group at 5,887.[^cite_note-229]

After World War II, an influx of [Appalachian migrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Americans) formed communities with children raised with acquired southern accents,[^cite_note-230] Along with [Lithuanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithuanian_Americans) in the World War II era, concentrating in the city's Southwest side in the [West Vernor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Vernor-Junction_Historic_District) area,[^cite_note-231] where the renovated Lithuanian Hall reopened in 2006.[^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233]

While African Americans in 2020 comprised 13.5% of Michigan's population, they made up nearly 77.2% of Detroit's population. The next largest population groups were non-Hispanic whites, at 10.1%, and Hispanics, at 8.0%.[^cite_note-2020CensusP2-234] In 2001, 103,000 Jews, or about 1.9% of the population, were living in the Detroit area.[^cite_note-235] According to the 2010 census, segregation in Detroit decreased in absolute and relative terms and in the first decade of the 21st century, about two-thirds of the total black population in the metropolitan area resided within the city limits of Detroit.[^cite_note-Wisely-236][^cite_note-237] The number of integrated neighborhoods increased from 100 in 2000 to 204 in 2010. After being ranked the most segregated metropolitan area in the United States in 2000, Detroit was ranked fourth most-segregated in 2010.[^cite_note-Wilkinson-238] A 2011 op-ed in *[The New York Times](30680.md)* attributed the decreased segregation rating to the overall exodus from the city, cautioning that these areas may soon become more segregated.&#x20;

There are four areas of Detroit with significant Asian and Asian American populations. Northeast Detroit has a large population of [Hmong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hmong_Americans)[^cite_note-Chou-239] with a smaller group of [Lao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laotian_Americans) people. A portion of Detroit next to eastern [Hamtramck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamtramck,_Michigan) includes [Bangladeshi Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladeshi_Americans), [Indian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Americans), and [Pakistani Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistani_Americans); nearly all of the Bangladeshi population in Detroit lives in that area. The area north of downtown has transient Asian national origin residents who are university students or hospital workers. Few of them have permanent residency after schooling ends. They are mostly Chinese and Indian but the population also includes Filipinos, Koreans, and Pakistanis. In Southwest and western Detroit there are smaller, scattered Asian communities.[^cite_note-MetzgerBoozap8-240][^cite_note-Archambault-241]

According to a 2014 study, 67% of the population of the city identified themselves as Christians, with 49% professing adherence to [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) churches, and 16% professing [Roman Catholic](606848.md) beliefs,[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243] while 24% claim [no religious affiliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion). Other religions collectively make up about 8% of the population.

### Age and gender



Population pyramid of Detroit in 2021

There was a wide distribution of age in the city, with 31.1% under the age of 18, 9.7% from 18 to 24, 29.5% from 25 to 44, 19.3% from 45 to 64, and 10.4% 65 years of age or older. The median age was 31 years. For every 100 females, there were 89.1 males. For every 100 females age 18 and over, there were 83.5 males.

### Households and income

Detroit's 639,111 residents represent 269,445 households, and 162,924 families residing in the city. Of the 269,445 households, 34.4% had children under the age of 18 living with them, 21.5% were married couples living together, 31.4% had a female householder with no husband present, 39.5% were non-families, 34.0% were made up of individuals, and 3.9% had someone living alone who was 65 years of age or older. The average household size was 2.59, and the average family size was 3.36.

The loss of industrial and working-class jobs in the city has resulted in high rates of poverty and associated problems.[^cite_note-time-256] From 2000 to 2009, the city's estimated median household income fell from \$29,526 to \$26,098.[^cite_note-257] As of 2010, the mean income of Detroit is below the overall U.S. average by several thousand dollars. Of every three Detroit residents, one lives in poverty. Luke Bergmann, author of *Getting Ghost: Two Young Lives and the Struggle for the Soul of an American City*, said in 2010, "Detroit is now one of the poorest big cities in the country".[^cite_note-258]

In the 2018 [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey), median household income in the city was \$31,283, compared with the median for Michigan of \$56,697.[^cite_note-259] The median income for a family was \$36,842, well below the state median of \$72,036.[^cite_note-260] 33.4% of families had income at or below the federally defined poverty level. Out of the total population, 47.3% of those under the age of 18 and 21.0% of those 65 and older had income at or below the federally defined poverty line.[^cite_note-261]

The city of Detroit has a higher than average percentage of households without a car. In 2016, 24.7% of Detroit households lacked a car, much higher than the national average of 8.7%. Detroit averaged 1.15 cars per household in 2016, compared to a national average of 1.8.[^cite_note-262]
